# THE VISION-CENTERED CARD SYSTEM

## A guide to seeing, attention, and situational reflection

This book presents a 52-card system with two meanings for every physical card: a primary state and a mirrored state. Together they form 104 ways of looking at a situation through the broader idea of **Vision**. The cards are not predictions and the situational details are not claims about what must be present. They are prompts: ways to notice people, actions, pressures, sensory qualities, relationships, and possible developments that might otherwise be overlooked.

Each entry has four layers. **Core meaning** explains the state itself. **Underlying pattern** preserves the calibrated semantic definition used to position the state in the system. **Situational lens** translates the state into everyday dimensions that can help direct attention. **Connections** shows how the card relates to its neighbor, mirror, direct opposite, and place in the larger circle.

When a situational dimension says **not strongly emphasized**, the card does not provide a useful association for that feature. This is preferable to inventing a detail simply to fill the space.

Each physical card has two semantic states. Hearts and Diamonds are **red primary / black mirrored**; Spades and Clubs are **black primary / red mirrored**.

## The center: Vision

Vision is the organized capacity by which a field becomes available to seeing, selection, and interpretation. It sits between openness and exclusion, motion and fixation, clarity and distortion: enough receptivity to register what is present, enough focus to distinguish figure from background, and enough continuity to maintain orientation as attention shifts. From this center, seeing can sharpen, diffuse, expand, narrow, stabilize, wander, reveal, or disappear. Vision is therefore not a particular image but the central condition from which different modes of perception become possible.

## How the deck is organized

Every card can be read in two states. Hearts and Diamonds show their primary state in red and their mirrored state in black; Spades and Clubs reverse that color order. A mirrored state is not merely an antonym. It is the corresponding state reflected across the semantic center of the system. A **Direct Opposite** is a separate relationship: it points to the coordinate across the underlying card surface rather than to the mirror of the same physical card.

The 104 states also form two long arcs. The primary arc passes through every primary state; the mirrored arc passes through the corresponding mirrored states. These arcs provide continuity from one state to the next, while mirror and Direct Opposite relationships allow the reader to look across the structure rather than only along it.

### Primary arc

A♥ Refocusing → A♦ Reframing → A♠ Constancy → A♣ Orienting → 2♣ Suppression → 2♠ Gating → 2♦ Accommodation → 2♥ Discovery → 3♥ Salience → 3♦ Calibration → 3♠ Stabilization → 3♣ Habit → 4♣ Retention → 4♠ Alignment → 4♦ Focusing → 4♥ Tracking → 5♥ Expansion → 5♦ Contrast → 5♠ Fusion → 5♣ Latency → 6♣ Afterimage → 6♠ Form → 6♦ Learning → 6♥ Pursuit → 7♥ Flow → 7♦ Recognition → 7♠ Pattern → 7♣ Inattention → 8♣ Gaze → 8♠ Homogeneity → 8♦ Adaptation → 8♥ Vividness → 9♥ Attention → 9♦ Saccade → 9♠ Coherence → 9♣ Lock → 10♣ Dimming → 10♠ Rest → 10♦ Tuning → 10♥ Scanning → J♥ Acuity → J♦ Processing → J♠ Persistence → J♣ Static → Q♣ Occlusion → Q♠ Continuity → Q♦ Flicker → Q♥ Fascination → K♥ Glare → K♦ Aberration → K♠ Fixation → K♣ Blindness

### Mirrored arc

A♥ Defocusing → A♦ Focus → A♠ Masking → A♣ Turn → 2♣ Fullness → 2♠ Noise → 2♦ Autofocus → 2♥ Familiarity → 3♥ Dulling → 3♦ Rivalry → 3♠ Glimpse → 3♣ Polarization → 4♣ Tilt → 4♠ Exploration → 4♦ Vigilance → 4♥ Aversion → 5♥ Disorientation → 5♦ Cueing → 5♠ Absorption → 5♣ Overlay → 6♣ Lag → 6♠ Restlessness → 6♦ Dissociation → 6♥ Presence → 7♥ Drift → 7♦ Derealization → 7♠ Ambiguity → 7♣ Reversal → 8♣ Recoil → 8♠ Awareness → 8♦ Certainty → 8♥ Distortion → 9♥ Habituation → 9♦ Steadfastness → 9♠ Streaking → 9♣ Constraint → 10♣ Resolution → 10♠ Approach → 10♦ Concentration → 10♥ Precision → J♥ Blur → J♦ Enhancement → J♠ Reorientation → J♣ Foresight → Q♣ Highlight → Q♠ Jitter → Q♦ Isolation → Q♥ Detachment → K♥ Saturation → K♦ Flare → K♠ Deprivation → K♣ Magnification

## Reading the situational lens

The situational lens is deliberately broad. It covers what may have triggered the situation, how a system or person responds, what feedback is present, what may follow, who is involved, what is being communicated, how attention and emotion are organized, what actions and movements matter, what kind of place or sensory field is suggested, and where the strongest obstacles or leverage points may lie. The associations come from the calibrated semantic representation of the card. They are best used as questions for observation rather than as facts to be forced onto a situation.

## Joker

The Joker represents the act of seeing the system as a whole rather than occupying one visual state. It integrates field, focus, context, movement, persistence, interruption, clarity, and uncertainty. In a reading, the Joker asks how the observer is organizing the act of seeing itself: what is being selected, what is being excluded, and how the current visual or interpretive frame might change.

<a id="card-ah"></a>
## A♥

### Primary — Refocusing (red A♥)

#### Core meaning

Refocusing is the deliberate redirection of seeing after attention has settled elsewhere. It changes what occupies the perceptual center without requiring the entire visual field to change. A newly dominant local feature or region can therefore reorganize attention around a fresh center.

#### Underlying pattern

Refocusing combines fresh reconfiguration before an arrangement settles with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Refocusing realizes this active change by displacing one attentional center with another, so movement in the field appears as directed selection rather than diffuse instability.

**Arc position.** Magnification → Refocusing → Reframing.

### Mirrored — Defocusing (black A♥)

#### Core meaning

Defocusing is the loosening of a previously selected focal point so that boundaries soften and peripheral information becomes more available. It reduces commitment to one visual target without eliminating perception itself. At its broadest, the release of focal structure approaches an undifferentiated field from which another center may later emerge.

#### Underlying pattern

Defocusing combines minimal boundary commitment with maximal availability of alternatives with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Defocusing realizes reflected disruption by releasing the dominance of one focal center; what is loosened becomes available to a wider, less bounded field rather than disappearing outright.

## A♦

### Primary — Reframing (red A♦)

#### Core meaning

Reframing is a change in the interpretive frame through which the same visual material is organized. The available input may remain similar while figure, background, scale, or context is understood differently.

#### Underlying pattern

Reframing combines fresh reconfiguration before an arrangement settles with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Reframing expresses change through reorganization: it alters relations among figure, ground, scale, and context while leaving much of the sensory material intact.

#### Situational lens

### Mirrored — Focus (black A♦)

#### Core meaning

Focus is the concentration of perceptual resources on a selected region or feature. It increases local clarity by reducing the relative influence of competing information.

#### Underlying pattern

Focus combines minimal boundary commitment with maximal availability of alternatives with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Focus restores a usable organizing center inside an otherwise open field, recovering local clarity without requiring the surrounding field to be erased.

## A♠

### Primary — Constancy (black A♠)

#### Core meaning

Constancy is the preservation of a stable percept despite changes in viewpoint, illumination, distance, or surrounding conditions. It allows an object or pattern to remain recognizably the same while its raw sensory presentation varies.

#### Underlying pattern

Constancy combines fresh reconfiguration before an arrangement settles with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Constancy places stability inside change: the sensory presentation may vary strongly while the represented object or relation remains recognizably continuous.

### Mirrored — Masking (red A♠)

#### Core meaning

Masking occurs when one visual signal reduces the visibility or discriminability of another. The obscured information may still be present, but access to it is weakened by competing input.

#### Underlying pattern

Masking combines minimal boundary commitment with maximal availability of alternatives with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Masking makes exclusion durable by allowing one signal to keep another below effective visibility even when the broader field remains available.

## A♣

### Primary — Orienting (black A♣)

#### Core meaning

Orienting is the shift of gaze or attention toward a potentially relevant stimulus. It establishes a direction for further seeing before detailed interpretation has occurred.

#### Underlying pattern

Orienting combines fresh reconfiguration before an arrangement settles with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Orienting converts change into an initial directional commitment, reducing indeterminacy by fixing a provisional axis from which later seeing proceeds.

### Mirrored — Turn (red A♣)

#### Core meaning

Turn is a redirection of perceptual orientation from one side, object, or frame toward another. It changes the direction of access while preserving the possibility of continued observation.

#### Underlying pattern

Turn combines minimal boundary commitment with maximal availability of alternatives with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Turn gives an open field a repeatable redirection, changing orientation while preserving continued access to what can be seen. Repeated turns broaden sampled coverage while preserving the same operation of redirection; as coverage accumulates, the field can become densely occupied.

#### Situational lens

<a id="card-2h"></a>
## 2♥

### Primary — Discovery (red 2♥)

#### Core meaning

Discovery is the emergence of a feature, relation, or object that was previously unnoticed or unavailable to attention. It converts latent visual information into an explicit perceptual possibility.

#### Underlying pattern

Discovery combines selective alteration within an otherwise recognizable field with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Discovery turns alteration into active novelty: attention encounters information that was latent in the field and promotes it into an explicit perceptual possibility. Discovery makes previously latent information explicit; once exposed, the newly available feature can acquire salience and begin to guide attention.

**Direct opposite.** Q♣ Occlusion.

**Place in the system.** Alteration × active perceptual energy.

**Arc position.** Accommodation → Discovery → Salience.

### Mirrored — Familiarity (black 2♥)

#### Core meaning

Familiarity is the recognition of a visual pattern as already encountered or known. It reduces novelty and allows perception to rely more heavily on established expectations.

#### Underlying pattern

Familiarity combines adaptability among several workable perceptual configurations with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Familiarity reduces the disruptive force of novelty by making a previously encountered pattern easier to recognize without fixing the whole field in one configuration. As familiarity increases, novelty loses some of its power to recruit attention, creating the conditions under which repeated input can become perceptually dull.

<a id="card-2d"></a>
## 2♦

### Primary — Accommodation (red 2♦)

#### Core meaning

Accommodation is an adjustment that brings a target into a more usable perceptual condition. In a broad visual sense it includes changes in optical focus, viewing strategy, or interpretive readiness that improve access to detail.

#### Underlying pattern

Accommodation combines selective alteration within an otherwise recognizable field with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Accommodation changes access by adjusting the perceptual apparatus or viewing strategy until previously imperfect detail becomes usable without rebuilding the whole scene. By improving access to weak or poorly resolved detail, accommodation can make previously unavailable structure discoverable.

### Mirrored — Autofocus (black 2♦)

#### Core meaning

Autofocus is an automatic convergence on a perceptually useful focal setting. It reduces the need for deliberate search by letting feedback select the clearest available configuration.

#### Underlying pattern

Autofocus combines adaptability among several workable perceptual configurations with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Autofocus restores local clarity through feedback, settling on a workable focal configuration without sustained deliberate control.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-2s"></a>
## 2♠

### Primary — Gating (black 2♠)

#### Core meaning

Gating is the selective admission of some visual information while other input is attenuated or excluded. It protects perceptual organization from being overwhelmed by everything that is simultaneously available.

#### Underlying pattern

Gating combines selective alteration within an otherwise recognizable field with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Gating stabilizes alteration by controlling access: a limited change in what is admitted can preserve coherence in the larger perceptual field. Once gating has selected which input is admitted, perceptual accommodation can adjust the system around that selected information without reopening the entire field.

### Mirrored — Noise (red 2♠)

#### Core meaning

Noise is visual variability that competes with the signal relevant to the current act of seeing. It can arise from the environment, the sensory pathway, or unstable interpretation and lowers confidence in discrimination.

#### Underlying pattern

Noise combines adaptability among several workable perceptual configurations with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Noise is adaptable variability that persists across the field; it keeps alternative fluctuations available but weakens the durability of any one clean signal.

## 2♣

### Primary — Suppression (black 2♣)

#### Core meaning

Suppression is the active reduction of a competing visual representation or response. It narrows what is allowed to dominate awareness and can stabilize a selected interpretation.

#### Underlying pattern

Suppression combines selective alteration within an otherwise recognizable field with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Suppression gives alteration an inertial bias by actively reducing competing representations, making the selected percept harder to dislodge.

### Mirrored — Fullness (red 2♣)

#### Core meaning

Fullness is a state in which the perceptual field is densely occupied, leaving little unused capacity for additional material. It can feel rich and complete, but it may also reduce the ease with which a new figure emerges.

#### Underlying pattern

Fullness combines adaptability among several workable perceptual configurations with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Fullness expresses adaptable consistency as dense occupancy: the field remains workable, but little unused capacity remains for a new figure to emerge. Dense occupancy is the accumulated condition in which further redirection encounters fewer unoccupied regions or unused attentional capacity.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-3h"></a>
## 3♥

### Primary — Salience (red 3♥)

#### Core meaning

Salience is the property by which a feature attracts perceptual priority relative to its surroundings. It makes one element stand out strongly enough to influence where attention moves next.

#### Underlying pattern

Salience combines deliberate local modification of priority, mapping, or organization with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Salience modifies priority rather than content itself, increasing the effective weight of one feature until it redirects attention and changes the next perceptual act. Salience is the perceptual weighting that allows a discovered feature to stand out strongly enough to influence the next act of seeing.

#### Situational lens

### Mirrored — Dulling (black 3♥)

#### Core meaning

Dulling is a reduction in perceptual intensity, distinctness, or responsiveness. Features remain available, but they exert less pull on attention and produce less differentiation from the background.

#### Underlying pattern

Dulling combines versatility that keeps multiple perceptual strategies or interpretations available with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Dulling often develops when repeated, familiar stimulation loses novelty, salience, and the power to recruit attention. Dulling reduces the dominance of one representation and can leave room for competing organizations to alternate for perceptual control.

## 3♦

### Primary — Calibration (red 3♦)

#### Core meaning

Calibration is the adjustment of perceptual thresholds or mappings so that judgments better correspond to current conditions. It aligns what is sensed with how the system interprets scale, contrast, position, or relevance.

#### Underlying pattern

Calibration combines deliberate local modification of priority, mapping, or organization with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Calibration modifies the mapping between input and judgment, correcting scale, threshold, contrast, or relevance so that subsequent interpretation better fits current conditions.

**Direct opposite.** J♠ Persistence.

**Place in the system.** Modification × perceptual reorganization.

**Arc position.** Salience → Calibration → Stabilization.

### Mirrored — Rivalry (black 3♦)

#### Core meaning

Rivalry is competition between incompatible visual organizations that cannot dominate simultaneously. Perception may alternate between them because the system cannot fully stabilize both at once.

#### Underlying pattern

Rivalry combines versatility that keeps multiple perceptual strategies or interpretations available with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Rivalry makes restoration unstable but versatile: incompatible organizations alternate for control, allowing perception to test more than one configuration without fully resolving the conflict. Rivalry becomes possible when no single representation remains strong enough to exclude its competitor completely.

#### Situational lens

## 3♠

### Primary — Stabilization (black 3♠)

#### Core meaning

Stabilization is the process by which a visual representation becomes less sensitive to momentary disturbance. It preserves a coherent object, scene, or relation long enough for reliable use.

#### Underlying pattern

Stabilization combines deliberate local modification of priority, mapping, or organization with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Stabilization is modification in service of continuity, reducing the influence of momentary disturbance until a representation can be used reliably across time.

### Mirrored — Glimpse (red 3♠)

#### Core meaning

A glimpse is a brief and incomplete availability of visual information. It provides enough structure to suggest a percept while leaving important details unresolved.

#### Underlying pattern

Glimpse combines versatility that keeps multiple perceptual strategies or interpretations available with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. A glimpse is a durable fragment rather than a complete percept; enough structure survives interruption to suggest an object while leaving the larger organization unresolved.

<a id="card-3c"></a>
## 3♣

### Primary — Habit (black 3♣)

#### Core meaning

Habit is the tendency for repeated ways of looking to become default perceptual routines. Familiar scanning paths, expectations, and selections can reduce effort while also making alternatives easier to miss.

#### Underlying pattern

Habit combines deliberate local modification of priority, mapping, or organization with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Habit turns repeated modification into perceptual inertia: familiar routes of looking become default, and those defaults leave recently selected patterns easier to retain than alternatives. Habit makes repeated selections easier to preserve, so familiar perceptual routines leave representations that are readily retained across small changes.

### Mirrored — Polarization (red 3♣)

#### Core meaning

Polarization is the separation of a visual or interpretive field into strongly opposed regions, categories, or tendencies. Intermediate possibilities become less influential as contrast between the poles increases.

#### Underlying pattern

Polarization combines versatility that keeps multiple perceptual strategies or interpretations available with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Polarization makes versatility consistent by repeatedly dividing the field into opposed organizations, reducing the influence of intermediate interpretations. When opposed alternatives dominate repeatedly, polarization can establish a stable directional bias in how the field is organized.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-4h"></a>
## 4♥

### Primary — Tracking (red 4♥)

#### Core meaning

Tracking is the maintenance of perceptual contact with a target as its position or surrounding conditions change. It requires continuous updating without losing the identity of what is being followed.

#### Underlying pattern

Tracking combines continuous adjustment in response to current conditions with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Tracking expresses active adjustment as continuous error correction between target motion and current gaze, preserving identity while location changes. Continuous tracking also samples the surrounding field around a moving target, so successful adjustment can gradually widen the context available for selection.

### Mirrored — Aversion (black 4♥)

#### Core meaning

Aversion is a tendency to withdraw gaze or attention from a stimulus experienced as undesirable, excessive, or threatening. It reorganizes seeing by reducing exposure to what would otherwise occupy the field.

#### Underlying pattern

Aversion combines resurgence of perceptual activity after weakening or interruption with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Aversion is resurgent disruption directed away from a stimulus: renewed perceptual activity takes the form of withdrawal rather than renewed engagement. Aversion is renewed perceptual activity directed away from the detected stimulus, converting readiness into active disengagement.

<a id="card-4d"></a>
## 4♦

### Primary — Focusing (red 4♦)

#### Core meaning

Focusing is the process of increasing the distinctness of a selected visual structure. It progressively reduces ambiguity until boundaries or features become sufficiently resolved for interpretation.

#### Underlying pattern

Focusing combines continuous adjustment in response to current conditions with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Focusing is adjustment through reorganization, progressively correcting ambiguity until the selected structure acquires sharper boundaries and a more usable internal relation. Focusing uses that registered structure to reduce ambiguity and increase the usable distinctness of selected detail.

### Mirrored — Vigilance (black 4♦)

#### Core meaning

Vigilance is sustained readiness to detect a relevant visual change. It keeps perceptual thresholds responsive over time even when the expected event is infrequent.

#### Underlying pattern

Vigilance combines resurgence of perceptual activity after weakening or interruption with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Vigilance restores readiness after quiet intervals by keeping detection thresholds responsive, allowing a weak or infrequent change to regain perceptual significance. Vigilance concentrates that sustained readiness on the possibility of a relevant change without requiring constant focal movement. Vigilance restores readiness to detect change; when the detected event is excessive or threatening, that readiness can immediately reorganize into aversive withdrawal.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-4s"></a>
## 4♠

### Primary — Alignment (black 4♠)

#### Core meaning

Alignment is the registration of visual elements into a shared orientation, position, or reference frame. Correct alignment allows relations among parts to be interpreted without unnecessary mismatch.

#### Underlying pattern

Alignment combines continuous adjustment in response to current conditions with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Alignment corrects mismatch while preserving the larger percept, bringing parts into a shared reference so that stability results from better registration rather than rigidity. Alignment uses persistent references to register changing parts within one stable frame. Once parts are aligned to a stable reference, focal reorganization can refine their boundaries and internal relations without losing registration.

### Mirrored — Exploration (red 4♠)

#### Core meaning

Exploration is active sampling of the visual field without committing too early to one interpretation or target. It increases coverage and can reveal alternatives that fixation would suppress.

#### Underlying pattern

Exploration combines resurgence of perceptual activity after weakening or interruption with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Exploration gives resurgence durable form by sustaining active sampling across alternatives instead of collapsing immediately into the first available interpretation. Sustained exploration keeps several regions available by maintaining detection readiness across a wider field. Exploration tests that displaced reference by sampling alternatives while preserving enough continuity to compare them.

<a id="card-4c"></a>
## 4♣

### Primary — Retention (black 4♣)

#### Core meaning

Retention is the persistence of a visual representation after the immediate input has weakened, shifted, or disappeared. It supports continuity by keeping recently seen structure available for comparison.

#### Underlying pattern

Retention combines continuous adjustment in response to current conditions with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Retention converts adjustment into continuity by keeping recently stabilized structure available after input changes, allowing habitual selections to leave usable traces. Retained structure provides a persistent reference against which later elements can be registered and aligned. Retention is the persistence of those selected representations after the immediate act of looking has moved on.

### Mirrored — Tilt (red 4♣)

#### Core meaning

Tilt is a deviation of orientation away from an expected vertical, horizontal, or reference direction. It changes the geometry of the perceived relation without necessarily changing the objects involved.

#### Underlying pattern

Tilt combines resurgence of perceptual activity after weakening or interruption with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Tilt expresses consistent resurgence as a repeatable deviation from a reference orientation, preserving organization while shifting its directional baseline. Tilt is that directional bias expressed as a displaced reference orientation rather than as a change in the objects themselves. A stable tilt can itself become the reference from which renewed exploration samples the surrounding field.

#### Situational lens

<a id="card-5h"></a>
## 5♥

### Primary — Expansion (red 5♥)

#### Core meaning

Expansion is the widening of the perceptual field, scale, or range of relevant visual information. It allows more context to enter awareness and changes what counts as the effective boundary of the scene.

#### Underlying pattern

Expansion combines coexisting variation that remains available for comparison with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Expansion expresses variation through active breadth, increasing the range of attended context so that more of the field can influence selection at once. Expansion is the resulting increase in contextual breadth, where a larger region of the field becomes relevant to active seeing.

### Mirrored — Disorientation (black 5♥)

#### Core meaning

Disorientation is the loss or weakening of a stable visual reference for position, direction, or relation. Information remains present, but the system has difficulty locating itself coherently within it.

#### Underlying pattern

Disorientation combines counteractive movement that compensates for an established tendency with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Disorientation is counteractive disruption of reference: several cues remain available, but their relations no longer support a single dependable location or direction.

<a id="card-5d"></a>
## 5♦

### Primary — Contrast (red 5♦)

#### Core meaning

Contrast is the difference between adjacent or competing visual values that makes boundaries and features discriminable. It converts variation into usable separation.

#### Underlying pattern

Contrast combines coexisting variation that remains available for comparison with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Contrast reorganizes variation into discriminable difference, converting gradients of intensity, color, or structure into boundaries that can guide interpretation.

### Mirrored — Cueing (black 5♦)

#### Core meaning

Cueing is the use of a signal to bias where attention, gaze, or interpretation should be directed next. A cue need not contain the target itself; it changes the probability of finding or recognizing it.

#### Underlying pattern

Cueing combines counteractive movement that compensates for an established tendency with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Cueing is counteractive restoration: when direct access is uncertain, a secondary signal compensates by biasing attention toward a location or interpretation likely to recover the target.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

## 5♠

### Primary — Fusion (black 5♠)

#### Core meaning

Fusion is the integration of multiple visual inputs into one coherent percept. It reduces duplication or conflict by treating separate signals as parts of a single representation.

#### Underlying pattern

Fusion combines coexisting variation that remains available for comparison with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Fusion stabilizes variation by integrating multiple signals into one coherent percept, preserving difference at the input while reducing conflict at the represented level.

### Mirrored — Absorption (red 5♠)

#### Core meaning

Absorption is deep capture by a perceptual object or scene so that competing input recedes from awareness. It produces strong continuity of engagement but may narrow responsiveness to alternatives.

#### Underlying pattern

Absorption combines counteractive movement that compensates for an established tendency with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Absorption makes counteractive engagement durable: competing material loses influence as one scene or object captures the perceptual system for an extended interval.

<a id="card-5c"></a>
## 5♣

### Primary — Latency (black 5♣)

#### Core meaning

Latency is the delay between the arrival of visual input and its effective registration, interpretation, or response. It represents a temporal gap rather than an absence of information.

#### Underlying pattern

Latency combines coexisting variation that remains available for comparison with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Latency expresses variation as delayed availability, making timing itself the resistant dimension between incoming information and effective registration or response. Delayed registration can leave residual traces, making latency the temporal condition from which an afterimage may persist after the source changes.

**Mirror — Overlay.** Across the Vision center, **Latency** reflects to **Overlay**. This is a same-card diameter relation rather than an adjacency on the circular path.

**Direct opposite.** 9♥ Attention.

**Place in the system.** Variation × perceptual inertia.

**Arc position.** Fusion → Latency → Afterimage.

### Mirrored — Overlay (red 5♣)

#### Core meaning

Overlay is the superimposition of one visual layer upon another. Multiple structures remain present at once, but their interaction can either enrich interpretation or obscure the underlying field.

#### Underlying pattern

Overlay combines counteractive movement that compensates for an established tendency with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Overlay makes counteraction persistent by maintaining multiple layers in the same region, so competing structures repeatedly influence one another instead of resolving cleanly. Superimposed layers increase temporal processing demands, so unresolved competition among overlays can appear as perceptual lag.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-6h"></a>
## 6♥

### Primary — Pursuit (red 6♥)

#### Core meaning

Pursuit is smooth, sustained following of a moving target. It maintains correspondence between gaze and motion so that the target remains relatively stable on the perceptual field.

#### Underlying pattern

Pursuit combines developing capability built through repeated perceptual experience with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Pursuit develops active control by converting repeated motion correction into a smooth correspondence between gaze and target, allowing dynamic following to become increasingly efficient.

#### Situational lens

**Arc position.** Learning → Pursuit → Flow.

### Mirrored — Presence (black 6♥)

#### Core meaning

Presence is the immediate occupation of awareness by what is visually here now. It minimizes distance between perception and its object, favoring direct availability over search or reconstruction.

#### Underlying pattern

Presence combines tautness created by sustained perceptual tension or expectancy with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Presence expresses disruptive tautness as immediate perceptual occupation: attention is held by what is here now, with little distance for search or reinterpretation. Presence can remain taut only while the current percept holds attention; when that holding force weakens, movement continues without a stable vector.

<a id="card-6d"></a>
## 6♦

### Primary — Learning (red 6♦)

#### Core meaning

Learning is a durable change in how visual information is selected, grouped, or interpreted as a result of experience. Repeated exposure changes future seeing by altering expectations and discrimination.

#### Underlying pattern

Learning combines developing capability built through repeated perceptual experience with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Learning develops reorganization across experience, changing the expectations and discriminations that will structure future seeing rather than merely improving one present judgment.

#### Situational lens

### Mirrored — Dissociation (black 6♦)

#### Core meaning

Dissociation is the separation of visual content from the broader context that would normally integrate it into a coherent experience. A scene may remain visible while feeling disconnected from its usual meaning or ownership.

#### Underlying pattern

Dissociation combines tautness created by sustained perceptual tension or expectancy with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Dissociation is taut restoration that fails to reintegrate context: visual content remains accessible, yet its usual relation to the surrounding scene or observer is weakened.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-6s"></a>
## 6♠

### Primary — Form (black 6♠)

#### Core meaning

Form is the organized shape or boundary that allows a visual object to be distinguished as a coherent whole. It provides structure without requiring the object to remain completely unchanged.

#### Underlying pattern

Form combines developing capability built through repeated perceptual experience with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Form develops stable organization by giving boundaries and relations a coherent shape that can survive moderate variation without becoming a different object.

### Mirrored — Restlessness (red 6♠)

#### Core meaning

Restlessness is unstable movement of gaze or attention that prevents a perceptual configuration from settling. The field remains active, but sustained inspection becomes difficult.

#### Underlying pattern

Restlessness combines tautness created by sustained perceptual tension or expectancy with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Restlessness makes tautness durable as repeated micro-shifts of gaze or attention; activation persists, but no single configuration is allowed to settle for long.

<a id="card-6c"></a>
## 6♣

### Primary — Afterimage (black 6♣)

#### Core meaning

An afterimage is a residual percept that persists after the initiating stimulus is removed or changed. It demonstrates that visual state can outlast immediate input and influence what is seen next.

#### Underlying pattern

Afterimage combines developing capability built through repeated perceptual experience with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Afterimage develops persistence from prior stimulation, allowing a representation to remain after the source has changed and making perceptual inertia visible as residual form. An afterimage is a developed residual of delayed registration: what arrived earlier remains perceptually active after the source has moved on.

### Mirrored — Lag (red 6♣)

#### Core meaning

Lag is delayed visual updating in which the representation follows changes more slowly than the underlying event. It creates temporal misalignment between the world and the current percept.

#### Underlying pattern

Lag combines tautness created by sustained perceptual tension or expectancy with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Lag is taut consistency expressed in time: the system continues to reproduce an earlier perceptual state after conditions have moved on, often following unresolved or layered input. Lag is the temporal persistence of an earlier layer or state after the visual field has already changed.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-7h"></a>
## 7♥

### Primary — Flow (red 7♥)

#### Core meaning

Flow is smooth continuity in the movement of gaze, attention, or visual organization. Transitions occur with little friction, allowing one perceptual state to lead naturally into the next.

#### Underlying pattern

Flow combines directed progress that carries an established capability forward with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Flow turns developed skill into perceptual progress, allowing active selection and movement to proceed with low friction while remaining responsive to the evolving field.

**Direct opposite.** 7♣ Inattention.

**Arc position.** Pursuit → Flow → Recognition.

### Mirrored — Drift (black 7♥)

#### Core meaning

Drift is gradual, often unintended movement of gaze, attention, or interpretation away from its previous position. It changes the perceptual center without a sharp transition.

#### Underlying pattern

Drift combines doldrums marked by weak directional pull and underdriven perceptual movement with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Drift expresses disruptive doldrums as movement without strong guidance: the field changes, but no stable target or direction organizes the motion. Drift is that underdriven continuation after focal tension has loosened, preserving movement while losing a guiding target.

<a id="card-7d"></a>
## 7♦

### Primary — Recognition (red 7♦)

#### Core meaning

Recognition is the successful matching of current visual input to an established object, pattern, or category. It turns sensed structure into something identified and usable.

#### Underlying pattern

Recognition combines directed progress that carries an established capability forward with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Recognition makes progress organizationally explicit by matching current structure to learned regularities, converting accumulated visual development into identification.

### Mirrored — Derealization (black 7♦)

#### Core meaning

Derealization is a perceptual condition in which the visible world remains present but feels distant, unreal, flattened, or strangely detached from ordinary familiarity. The problem is not simple invisibility but altered integration of what is seen.

#### Underlying pattern

Derealization combines doldrums marked by weak directional pull and underdriven perceptual movement with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Derealization is restoration without full reintegration: recognizable structure can return while its ordinary sense of contextual reality or familiarity remains weakened.

#### Situational lens

<a id="card-7s"></a>
## 7♠

### Primary — Pattern (black 7♠)

#### Core meaning

Pattern is a recurring organization of visual elements whose relations are stable enough to be detected across instances. It gives structure to repetition and supports prediction.

#### Underlying pattern

Pattern combines directed progress that carries an established capability forward with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Pattern gives progress a stable grammar, allowing repeated relations to be detected across different instances and used as a dependable basis for prediction.

### Mirrored — Ambiguity (red 7♠)

#### Core meaning

Ambiguity occurs when available visual information supports more than one plausible organization. The field cannot be reduced to a single stable interpretation without additional evidence or commitment.

#### Underlying pattern

Ambiguity combines doldrums marked by weak directional pull and underdriven perceptual movement with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Ambiguity is durable underdetermination: several interpretations remain viable through continued viewing, so uncertainty persists instead of resolving into one dominant form.

<a id="card-7c"></a>
## 7♣

### Primary — Inattention (black 7♣)

#### Core meaning

Inattention is the failure to allocate sufficient perceptual resources to available information. A feature may be physically visible yet functionally absent because it never becomes selected for processing.

#### Underlying pattern

Inattention combines directed progress that carries an established capability forward with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Inattention is progress constrained by inertia: the visual system continues operating, but unattended material receives too little priority to redirect the established course. Inattention preserves the current course by withholding resources from alternatives, so gaze can remain directed elsewhere for increasingly long intervals.

**Mirror — Reversal.** Across the Vision center, **Inattention** reflects to **Reversal**. This is a same-card diameter relation rather than an adjacency on the circular path.

**Direct opposite.** 7♥ Flow.

**Place in the system.** Progress × perceptual inertia.

**Arc position.** Pattern → Inattention → Gaze.

### Mirrored — Reversal (red 7♣)

#### Core meaning

Reversal is a change in perceived direction, ordering, polarity, or relation such that an established orientation is inverted. It preserves much of the same material while altering how its parts correspond.

#### Underlying pattern

Reversal combines doldrums marked by weak directional pull and underdriven perceptual movement with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Reversal makes weak directional movement consistent by repeatedly exchanging the current orientation for its contrary, producing change without a stable forward tendency. Repeated reversal establishes a withdrawal-ready pattern in which a new disturbance can trigger recoil rather than continued approach.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-8h"></a>
## 8♥

### Primary — Vividness (red 8♥)

#### Core meaning

Vividness is the experienced richness, intensity, and immediacy of a visual representation. It increases the felt presence of detail without necessarily increasing objective accuracy.

#### Underlying pattern

Vividness combines iterative evolution that changes how the field operates over time with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Vividness expresses evolution through intensity, making developed visual distinctions feel more present and consequential as the perceptual system becomes increasingly responsive to them. Vividness increases the effective strength of available distinctions, making them more likely to recruit selective attention.

**Place in the system.** Evolution × active perceptual energy.

**Arc position.** Adaptation → Vividness → Attention.

### Mirrored — Distortion (black 8♥)

#### Core meaning

Distortion is a systematic alteration of visual form, scale, position, or relation away from the configuration that generated the input. It changes how information is represented rather than simply making it weaker.

#### Underlying pattern

Distortion combines fragility in which organization remains available but is easily displaced with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Distortion expresses fragile disruption by altering geometry, proportion, or relation in ways that can destabilize a percept that otherwise remains available. Repeated distortion can become predictable enough that its disruptive force weakens with exposure.

<a id="card-8d"></a>
## 8♦

### Primary — Adaptation (red 8♦)

#### Core meaning

Adaptation is a change in visual sensitivity or interpretation produced by sustained exposure to particular conditions. It recalibrates perception so that a persistent input exerts less disruptive influence.

#### Underlying pattern

Adaptation combines iterative evolution that changes how the field operates over time with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Adaptation is evolution through recalibration, changing sensitivity and interpretation so that persistent conditions become less disruptive and more efficiently represented.

#### Situational lens

### Mirrored — Certainty (black 8♦)

#### Core meaning

Certainty is strong commitment to one perceptual interpretation with little experienced need for alternatives. It can stabilize action, but excessive certainty can make contradictory evidence harder to integrate.

#### Underlying pattern

Certainty combines fragility in which organization remains available but is easily displaced with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Certainty is fragile restoration when commitment outruns flexibility: one interpretation becomes strongly available, but its apparent stability can break sharply when contradictory evidence accumulates.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-8s"></a>
## 8♠

### Primary — Homogeneity (black 8♠)

#### Core meaning

Homogeneity is low internal variation across a visual field or group of elements. It reduces local contrast and encourages the field to be treated as a uniform whole.

#### Underlying pattern

Homogeneity combines iterative evolution that changes how the field operates over time with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Homogeneity evolves stability toward uniformity, reducing internal difference until the field is more likely to be processed as one continuous region than as many separate elements.

### Mirrored — Awareness (red 8♠)

#### Core meaning

Awareness is the availability of visual content for conscious access, report, or deliberate use. It is broader than focal attention because something may be present in awareness without occupying the center.

#### Underlying pattern

Awareness combines fragility in which organization remains available but is easily displaced with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Awareness is fragile durability of availability: content remains accessible for report or use, yet its presence does not guarantee that attention or interpretation will remain fixed.

<a id="card-8c"></a>
## 8♣

### Primary — Gaze (black 8♣)

#### Core meaning

Gaze is the sustained direction of the eyes and attention toward a region, object, or scene. It creates persistence of access and can deepen inspection through time.

#### Underlying pattern

Gaze combines iterative evolution that changes how the field operates over time with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Gaze evolves inertia into sustained access, maintaining a selected region long enough for repeated inspection while making large redirections progressively less likely. Gaze is sustained selection made visible in eye position: one region continues to receive access while alternatives remain comparatively unattended.

### Mirrored — Recoil (red 8♣)

#### Core meaning

Recoil is a rapid withdrawal of gaze or attention after an intense, surprising, or aversive visual event. It reverses approach and increases distance from the stimulus.

#### Underlying pattern

Recoil combines fragility in which organization remains available but is easily displaced with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Recoil gives fragile perception a consistent withdrawal response: an intense or aversive event repeatedly drives gaze or attention away from the triggering region. Recoil is the fragile continuation of that reversed direction as a rapid, repeatable withdrawal from the triggering region.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-9h"></a>
## 9♥

### Primary — Attention (red 9♥)

#### Core meaning

Attention is the selective allocation of perceptual resources to some information over other available information. It determines what receives priority for further processing and action.

#### Underlying pattern

Attention combines a shift in perceptual weighting, reference, or sampling position with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Attention expresses shift through active weighting, relocating perceptual resources so that one region or feature gains priority over alternatives without requiring a new sensory field. Attention converts that perceptual strength into prioritized processing by allocating resources to the most consequential region or feature.

**Arc position.** Vividness → Attention → Saccade.

### Mirrored — Habituation (black 9♥)

#### Core meaning

Habituation is a reduction in response to a visual event after repeated exposure without important consequence. What was once attention-grabbing becomes increasingly easy to ignore.

#### Underlying pattern

Habituation combines fallenness in which perceptual organization has become degraded or displaced with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Habituation expresses disruptive fallenness as a loss of attentional pull: repeated input remains present but progressively loses its ability to recruit response. Habituation is the resulting fall in response when a recurring distortion or event no longer carries sufficient novelty or consequence to recruit attention.

<a id="card-9d"></a>
## 9♦

### Primary — Saccade (red 9♦)

#### Core meaning

A saccade is a rapid shift of gaze that relocates the high-resolution center of vision from one point to another. It is a discrete reorganization of sampling rather than smooth following.

#### Underlying pattern

Saccade combines a shift in perceptual weighting, reference, or sampling position with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. A saccade is reorganization by discrete shift, rapidly moving the high-resolution sampling center and forcing the scene to be reconstructed around a new fixation.

### Mirrored — Steadfastness (black 9♦)

#### Core meaning

Steadfastness is the maintenance of a perceptual or attentional commitment despite distraction or competing signals. It favors continuity of inspection over frequent redirection.

#### Underlying pattern

Steadfastness combines fallenness in which perceptual organization has become degraded or displaced with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Steadfastness is restorative fallenness resisted by commitment: despite distraction or degradation, the system repeatedly returns resources to the selected target and preserves a workable orientation.

#### Situational lens

<a id="card-9s"></a>
## 9♠

### Primary — Coherence (black 9♠)

#### Core meaning

Coherence is the degree to which visual parts fit together into a mutually supporting whole. A coherent percept has relations that reinforce rather than contradict one another.

#### Underlying pattern

Coherence combines a shift in perceptual weighting, reference, or sampling position with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Coherence stabilizes a shifted field by making its parts mutually supportive, allowing a changed viewpoint or arrangement to remain interpretable as one connected whole.

### Mirrored — Streaking (red 9♠)

#### Core meaning

Streaking is the elongation or smearing of visual information along a direction, often associated with motion or temporal integration. It preserves a trace of trajectory while reducing precise localization.

#### Underlying pattern

Streaking combines fallenness in which perceptual organization has become degraded or displaced with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Streaking is durable degradation along a trajectory: positional information spreads across time or motion, preserving direction while sacrificing exact localization.

<a id="card-9c"></a>
## 9♣

### Primary — Lock (black 9♣)

#### Core meaning

Lock is rigid maintenance of gaze, attention, or interpretation on a selected target. It maximizes persistence but reduces the ease of shifting to alternatives.

#### Underlying pattern

Lock combines a shift in perceptual weighting, reference, or sampling position with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Lock makes shift resistant by holding gaze, attention, or interpretation on one target even when the surrounding field offers competing reasons to move. When a locked target dominates for long intervals, responsiveness to the surrounding field can diminish even though the target itself remains selected. Lock also reduces the effective influence of surrounding information; as the selected target monopolizes access, the rest of the field can become functionally dimmer.

**Place in the system.** Shift × perceptual inertia.

### Mirrored — Constraint (red 9♣)

#### Core meaning

Constraint is a restriction on the range of possible gaze positions, interpretations, or visual actions. It narrows the field of available transitions without necessarily eliminating perception.

#### Underlying pattern

Constraint combines fallenness in which perceptual organization has become degraded or displaced with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Constraint makes fallenness consistent by limiting available transitions, preserving a narrowed range of gaze positions or interpretations after broader flexibility has been lost. By narrowing possible transitions, constraint can concentrate the remaining perceptual field into a smaller range where local discrimination becomes more consequential.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-10h"></a>
## 10♥

### Primary — Scanning (red 10♥)

#### Core meaning

Scanning is systematic movement through the visual field in search of relevant information. It trades continuous fixation for structured coverage.

#### Underlying pattern

Scanning combines overall stasis with limited internal activity with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Scanning keeps active energy alive inside stasis, moving methodically across a largely stable field so that search continues without requiring the environment itself to change. As systematic scanning narrows to smaller differences, successful search depends increasingly on acuity rather than on breadth alone.

**Mirror — Precision.** Across the Vision center, **Scanning** reflects to **Precision**. This is a same-card diameter relation rather than an adjacency on the circular path.

**Direct opposite.** 4♣ Retention.

**Place in the system.** Stasis × active perceptual energy.

**Arc position.** Tuning → Scanning → Acuity.

### Mirrored — Precision (black 10♥)

#### Core meaning

Precision is the narrowness and repeatability with which a visual feature can be localized, discriminated, or represented. It concerns exactness of resolution rather than breadth of seeing.

#### Underlying pattern

Precision combines downspin in which loss of breadth, clarity, or control becomes self-reinforcing with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Precision is disruptive downspin narrowed into exactness: as the available field contracts, localization can become highly repeatable within the remaining range even while breadth is lost.

**Direct opposite.** 4♣ Tilt.

**Place in the system.** Downspin × perceptual disruption.

## 10♦

### Primary — Tuning (red 10♦)

#### Core meaning

Tuning is the adjustment of perceptual sensitivity toward a particular range of features or conditions. It improves selectivity by changing what the system responds to most strongly.

#### Underlying pattern

Tuning combines overall stasis with limited internal activity with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Tuning reorganizes a largely stable field by narrowing sensitivity toward selected features, improving discrimination without requiring broad exploratory movement.

### Mirrored — Concentration (black 10♦)

#### Core meaning

Concentration is sustained, narrow allocation of attention to a selected visual task or target. It reduces interruption and deepens processing within a restricted region.

#### Underlying pattern

Concentration combines downspin in which loss of breadth, clarity, or control becomes self-reinforcing with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Concentration is restorative downspin through narrowing: depleted breadth is compensated by sustained investment in a restricted target or task.

#### Situational lens

## 10♠

### Primary — Rest (black 10♠)

#### Core meaning

Rest is a low-movement perceptual state in which active search is suspended while basic readiness is preserved. It creates stability without requiring complete disengagement.

#### Underlying pattern

Rest combines overall stasis with limited internal activity with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Rest is stable stasis rather than absence, suspending search while preserving enough organization and readiness for seeing to resume without rebuilding the perceptual field.

### Mirrored — Approach (red 10♠)

#### Core meaning

Approach is movement of gaze, attention, or viewpoint toward a visual target. It decreases perceptual distance and increases the opportunity for detail to become available.

#### Underlying pattern

Approach combines downspin in which loss of breadth, clarity, or control becomes self-reinforcing with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Approach gives downspin a durable direction by moving perceptual access closer to a target even when the wider field has lost energy or organization.

<a id="card-10c"></a>
## 10♣

### Primary — Dimming (black 10♣)

#### Core meaning

Dimming is a reduction in the apparent intensity or accessibility of visual information. The field remains present but exerts less perceptual force.

#### Underlying pattern

Dimming combines overall stasis with limited internal activity with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Dimming expresses stasis through inertia by lowering the effective intensity of the field, making further redirection less likely without removing visual information altogether. Dimming can therefore occur in a field that remains comparatively fixed while its effective intensity and power to redirect attention decline. Dimming preserves that narrowed organization while lowering the surrounding field's ability to compete for attention.

### Mirrored — Resolution (red 10♣)

#### Core meaning

Resolution is the capacity to distinguish fine spatial or structural differences within a visual field. Higher resolution separates details that would otherwise merge.

#### Underlying pattern

Resolution combines downspin in which loss of breadth, clarity, or control becomes self-reinforcing with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Resolution makes downspin consistent by concentrating remaining capacity into separable detail, preserving local discrimination while the broader field becomes more restricted. Resolution benefits from such narrowing by devoting available capacity to fine distinctions within the remaining field.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

<a id="card-jh"></a>
## J♥

### Primary — Acuity (red J♥)

#### Core meaning

Acuity is the fineness of visual discrimination, especially the ability to resolve closely spaced features as distinct. It describes sharpness of discrimination rather than the amount of information attended.

#### Underlying pattern

Acuity combines inertia that preserves an established perceptual organization with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Acuity is energetic inertia at a fine scale: discrimination remains strongly active but increasingly committed to preserving sharp local distinctions rather than seeking new organization. Acuity supplies sharply differentiated input that can then be grouped, compared, and interpreted by subsequent visual processing.

### Mirrored — Blur (black J♥)

#### Core meaning

Blur is the loss of fine boundary and detail information, causing nearby features to merge or spread. It weakens precise localization while leaving broader form potentially recognizable.

#### Underlying pattern

Blur combines formlessness produced by loss of stable boundaries or organizing shape with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Blur is disruptive formlessness at the level of boundaries, spreading local detail until neighboring features merge while broader visual presence remains available.

<a id="card-jd"></a>
## J♦

### Primary — Processing (red J♦)

#### Core meaning

Processing is the sequence of operations that transforms visual input into organized representations suitable for recognition, judgment, or action. It includes selection, grouping, comparison, and integration rather than a single event.

#### Underlying pattern

Processing combines inertia that preserves an established perceptual organization with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Processing reorganizes inertial input by repeatedly transforming selected signals into grouped, compared, and interpretable representations. Processing preserves and reorganizes fine distinctions rather than treating them as undifferentiated visual intensity. Repeated processing can stabilize the representation it produces, allowing organized content to persist through small disturbances.

**Direct opposite.** 3♠ Stabilization.

**Place in the system.** Inertia × perceptual reorganization.

**Arc position.** Acuity → Processing → Persistence.

### Mirrored — Enhancement (black J♦)

#### Core meaning

Enhancement is selective amplification of visual information so that relevant features become easier to detect or discriminate. It increases usable signal without necessarily changing the underlying scene.

#### Underlying pattern

Enhancement combines formlessness produced by loss of stable boundaries or organizing shape with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Enhancement is restorative formlessness constrained into usable signal, amplifying selected structure so that organization can re-emerge from weak or diffuse information.

#### Situational lens

## J♠

### Primary — Persistence (black J♠)

#### Core meaning

Persistence is the continuation of a visual representation or attentional state despite interruption or disturbance. It provides temporal stability and prevents every small change from resetting perception.

#### Underlying pattern

Persistence combines inertia that preserves an established perceptual organization with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Persistence is stability under inertia, allowing representations to survive interruption and preventing minor disturbances from forcing a complete perceptual reset. Persistence is the stable continuation of an organized representation after processing has established it.

### Mirrored — Reorientation (red J♠)

#### Core meaning

Reorientation is the establishment of a new perceptual reference after attention, gaze, or spatial understanding has been disrupted. It restores a workable direction without simply returning to the old one.

#### Underlying pattern

Reorientation combines formlessness produced by loss of stable boundaries or organizing shape with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Reorientation gives formlessness durable recovery by establishing a new reference direction after the old organizing frame has broken down.

## J♣

### Primary — Static (black J♣)

#### Core meaning

Static is a condition in which the visual field or its interpretation changes very little across time. Stability is high, but the absence of updating can also make new information harder to register.

#### Underlying pattern

Static combines inertia that preserves an established perceptual organization with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Static is inertia made self-reinforcing: little changes across time, so the current visual arrangement becomes easier to maintain than to revise.

**Direct opposite.** 3♥ Salience.

**Place in the system.** Inertia × perceptual inertia.

**Arc position.** Persistence → Static → Occlusion.

### Mirrored — Foresight (red J♣)

#### Core meaning

Foresight is anticipatory visual interpretation based on patterns that imply what is likely to appear or occur next. It uses present structure to prepare perception for future information.

#### Underlying pattern

Foresight combines formlessness produced by loss of stable boundaries or organizing shape with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Foresight makes formless or incomplete information consistent by using recurring patterns to prepare perception for likely future structure. Foresight gives incomplete structure a repeatable expectation, predisposing perception to emphasize features that match the anticipated pattern.

#### Situational lens

<a id="card-qh"></a>
## Q♥

### Primary — Fascination (red Q♥)

#### Core meaning

Fascination is unusually strong and sustained capture of attention by a visual object or scene. It draws resources toward the stimulus with little effort and can resist competing demands.

#### Underlying pattern

Fascination combines standstill in which persistence is high and effective redirection is low with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Fascination is energetic standstill: attention remains highly activated but becomes captured by one object or scene, leaving little effective movement toward alternatives.

**Mirror — Detachment.** Across the Vision center, **Fascination** reflects to **Detachment**. This is a same-card diameter relation rather than an adjacency on the circular path.

**Direct opposite.** 2♣ Suppression.

**Place in the system.** Standstill × active perceptual energy.

**Arc position.** Flicker → Fascination → Glare.

### Mirrored — Detachment (black Q♥)

#### Core meaning

Detachment is reduced perceptual or emotional investment in what is seen. The scene remains available, but it exerts less pull on attention and interpretation.

#### Underlying pattern

Detachment combines unknowability in which available evidence does not support one dependable interpretation with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Detachment expresses disruptive unknowability by loosening investment in a percept that remains available but insufficiently compelling to organize attention. Detachment and saturation both reduce responsiveness to continuing stimulation, the former through disengagement and the latter through over-occupation of the response range.

## Q♦

### Primary — Flicker (red Q♦)

#### Core meaning

Flicker is rapid variation in visual intensity or presence across time. It repeatedly interrupts stability while remaining localized to a recurring signal.

#### Underlying pattern

Flicker combines standstill in which persistence is high and effective redirection is low with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Flicker is reorganization at standstill: the same localized signal repeatedly changes in temporal availability, preventing complete stability without creating a new spatial organization.

### Mirrored — Isolation (black Q♦)

#### Core meaning

Isolation is the separation of a visual element from the context that would normally connect it to surrounding structure. It can improve inspection of the element while weakening relational meaning.

#### Underlying pattern

Isolation combines unknowability in which available evidence does not support one dependable interpretation with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Isolation is restorative unknowability through separation, making one element inspectable by detaching it from relationships that would otherwise complicate interpretation.

#### Situational lens

## Q♠

### Primary — Continuity (black Q♠)

#### Core meaning

Continuity is the preservation of visual identity or organization across interruptions, movement, or partial change. It allows separate moments of input to be experienced as belonging to one ongoing percept.

#### Underlying pattern

Continuity combines standstill in which persistence is high and effective redirection is low with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Continuity is stable standstill across interruption, preserving identity so effectively that separate moments or partial views are experienced as one ongoing percept.

### Mirrored — Jitter (red Q♠)

#### Core meaning

Jitter is small, rapid instability in position, orientation, or visual registration. The underlying target remains present, but its representation fails to settle completely.

#### Underlying pattern

Jitter combines unknowability in which available evidence does not support one dependable interpretation with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Jitter is durable unknowability at small scale: the target persists, but rapid positional instability prevents the representation from settling into a fully dependable location.

## Q♣

### Primary — Occlusion (black Q♣)

#### Core meaning

Occlusion is the blocking of one visual object or region by another. It removes direct access to part of the field while often leaving enough context to infer what continues behind the obstruction.

#### Underlying pattern

Occlusion combines standstill in which persistence is high and effective redirection is low with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Occlusion is inertial standstill through blocked access, preventing new direct information from reaching part of the field while the hidden structure is inferred from what remains.

### Mirrored — Highlight (red Q♣)

#### Core meaning

Highlight is the deliberate increase of perceptual prominence for a selected feature or region. It changes relative salience so that one element becomes easier to notice or compare.

#### Underlying pattern

Highlight combines unknowability in which available evidence does not support one dependable interpretation with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Highlight makes uncertain information consistently prominent by increasing the relative visibility of one feature even when the wider context remains unresolved. Highlighting makes such expected or uncertain features consistently prominent enough to guide interpretation.

<a id="card-kh"></a>
## K♥

### Primary — Glare (red K♥)

#### Core meaning

Glare is excessive brightness or scattered light that overwhelms useful contrast. More light is present, but the ability to discriminate important visual structure is reduced.

#### Underlying pattern

Glare combines stagnation in which further useful perceptual change is strongly blocked with active attention, intensity, motion, or directed visual effort. Glare is energetic stagnation: intensity remains high but useful discrimination collapses, so additional visual energy reinforces impairment rather than producing new information.

### Mirrored — Saturation (black K♥)

#### Core meaning

Saturation is a condition in which increasing visual intensity or stimulation no longer produces proportionate differentiation because the available response range is already heavily occupied. It represents perceptual excess rather than simple richness.

#### Underlying pattern

Saturation combines lethargy marked by minimal responsiveness and depleted capacity for further perceptual change with loosening, dispersal, interruption, or reversal of established selection. Saturation is the endpoint of that excess, where additional stimulation adds occupancy but little new differentiation or engagement. Once the response range is saturated, additional stimulation no longer restores engagement or produces proportionate differentiation. Saturation leaves little unused response range for additional stimulation, so intense light tends to spread rather than produce proportionate new differentiation.

<a id="card-kd"></a>
## K♦

### Primary — Aberration (red K♦)

#### Core meaning

Aberration is a systematic deviation in the formation or interpretation of visual structure, producing displacement, deformation, or color error. It changes the geometry of the percept in a repeatable way.

#### Underlying pattern

Aberration combines stagnation in which further useful perceptual change is strongly blocked with reframing, recalibration, recoding, or relational reorganization. Aberration is reorganization at stagnation: the visual system continues to transform input, but the transformation is systematically misregistered, so further processing reproduces error rather than resolving it.
