### Mirrored — Flare (black K♦)

#### Core meaning

Flare is the spread of bright light beyond its source, reducing local contrast and extending illumination into neighboring regions. It can reveal intensity while obscuring fine structure.

#### Underlying pattern

Flare combines lethargy marked by minimal responsiveness and depleted capacity for further perceptual change with recovery, recalibration, or reconstruction of usable organization. Flare is restorative lethargy expressed paradoxically as spread light: illumination expands beyond its source, but depleted contrast limits how much usable structure the extra light restores. Flare is that spread of intense light into neighboring regions, where added illumination fails to restore fine contrast because the response is already heavily occupied.

## K♠

### Primary — Fixation (black K♠)

#### Core meaning

Fixation is stable maintenance of gaze or perceptual commitment on a selected point or interpretation. It supports detailed inspection but becomes restrictive when shifting is required.

#### Underlying pattern

Fixation combines stagnation in which further useful perceptual change is strongly blocked with coherence, integration, continuity, or resistance to disturbance. Fixation is stable stagnation, preserving one gaze point or interpretation so strongly that the stability itself becomes the main barrier to perceptual change.

### Mirrored — Deprivation (red K♠)

#### Core meaning

Deprivation is insufficient visual input or access for normal discrimination and updating. The system is forced to rely more heavily on memory, expectation, or nonvisual information.

#### Underlying pattern

Deprivation combines lethargy marked by minimal responsiveness and depleted capacity for further perceptual change with durability through disturbance, even when organization is incomplete or strained. Deprivation is durable lethargy through insufficient input: the reduced field persists, forcing perception to rely on memory, expectation, or sparse residual cues.

<a id="card-kc"></a>
## K♣

### Primary — Blindness (black K♣)

#### Core meaning

Blindness is the absence or functional unavailability of usable visual information within the relevant field. It is the limiting condition in which seeing can no longer guide discrimination there. When no reliable figure can be selected, focal commitment loses its object and the field becomes effectively undifferentiated.

#### Underlying pattern

Blindness combines stagnation in which further useful perceptual change is strongly blocked with delay, suppression, fixation, occlusion, or resistance to redirection. Blindness is perceptual stagnation at the inertial limit: no usable visual discrimination can redirect the field, and failed focal structure collapses into unavailable or undifferentiated information.

**Mirror — Magnification.** Across the Vision center, **Blindness** reflects to **Magnification**. This is a same-card diameter relation rather than an adjacency on the circular path.

**Direct opposite.** A♥ Refocusing.

**Place in the system.** Stagnation × perceptual inertia.

**Arc position.** Fixation → Blindness → Defocusing.

### Mirrored — Magnification (red K♣)

#### Core meaning

Magnification is the enlargement of a selected visual region or feature relative to the surrounding field. It sacrifices breadth to increase access to local detail. By making one region dominate available detail, magnification creates a strong candidate around which a new focal center can be established.

#### Underlying pattern

Magnification combines lethargy marked by minimal responsiveness and depleted capacity for further perceptual change with repeatable tendencies that reinforce a dependable perceptual direction. Magnification expresses lethargic consistency by concentrating limited perceptual capacity onto a restricted region; breadth decreases while the enlarged area remains persistently available.

#### Situational lens

Use the following associations to focus observation and thought. They describe what this state most readily brings forward; they are not predictions or required features of a situation.

#### Connections

**Moving toward Refocusing.** Magnification moves toward **Refocusing** when its present organization begins to give way to the next visual condition. Refocusing is the deliberate redirection of seeing after attention has settled elsewhere.

**Mirror — Blindness.** Across the Vision center, **Magnification** reflects to **Blindness**. This is a same-card diameter relation rather than an adjacency on the circular path.

**Direct opposite.** A♥ Defocusing.

**Place in the system.** Lethargy × perceptual consistency.

# Appendix: How the semantic calibration is used

The card names and prose above are organized by a calibrated semantic model. For each state, the **Core meaning** and **Underlying pattern** are the canonical text used to represent that state. The situational lens is calculated afterward and is not fed back into the card representation. This separation matters: otherwise repeated labels such as “color,” “risk,” or “trigger” could make unrelated cards look artificially similar.

The situational lens uses 162 dimensions. Within each dimension, the system compares the card state with possible descriptive values and keeps the strongest associations when the signal is meaningful. Weak results are shown as **not strongly emphasized** rather than being converted into invented detail. The result should be read as a structured field of prompts for attention, not as evidence that a person, object, color, animal, place, or event is objectively present.

The deck has three different kinds of relationship. A **mirror** connects the two states of the same physical card across the Vision center. A **Direct Opposite** connects opposite coordinates on the underlying semantic surface. An **arc neighbor** is simply the state immediately before or after another state along one of the two long paths through the deck. Keeping those relationships separate is essential to the internal geometry of the system.
